From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m here because I keep dropping the ball on things I’m supposed to remember. Like scheduled callbacks, work orders, follow-ups I told people I’d handle—basic stuff that should be routine. And it’s not just one or two things anymore, it’s piled up.\n\nAt work, if somebody asks me to call back or check on something, I really mean to do it, but then it’s like it disappears unless it’s right in front of me. Then later someone says, “Did you ever follow up on that?” and I get that sinking feeling because I know I forgot again. It’s gotten bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I ruin my chance at moving up. I’ve been trying to get ahead, and now I’m worried I’m going to lose that shot because I can’t keep track of what I promised people.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety have you been feeling because of these memory issues?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I probably want to admit. It’s not just, “Oh, I forgot something and I feel bad.” It’s turned into this constant knot in my stomach because now there are actual consequences stacking up.\n\nI’ve got a backlog at work that I keep trying to catch up on, but then more stuff gets added and I miss another callback or forget to close something out, and then tenants complain because they’re waiting on me or they think I ignored them. And honestly, I don’t blame them, but it makes me feel awful because I’m not trying to blow anyone off.\n\nThe worst part is it’s on my record now. There was a safety review tied to something I missed, and that scared me. Like, this isn’t just annoying anymore — it makes me look unreliable. I’ve been trying to move up, trying to show I can handle more responsibility, and now I’m worried I’ve ruined that. I keep thinking, if they’re already watching me for mistakes, why would they ever promote me?\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the anxiety is pretty high. I feel embarrassed, stressed, and kind of scared that all the work I’ve put in is going to get wiped out because I can’t keep track of things the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not new. I mean, the work stuff has made it a lot more obvious and a lot more serious, but if I’m honest, I remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things, but I was always the kid who misplaced papers or didn’t turn something in because I couldn’t find it, or I’d miss one part of the directions even though I thought I was listening. I needed reminders to finish classwork, not because I didn’t care, but because I’d get off track or lose where I was in the assignment.\n\nSo… yeah, this job pressure has made it feel worse, but the remembering and keeping track of things part has been there a long time. I just used to think it was me being careless or scattered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still or is always bouncing around. I might shift in my chair or mess with something in my hands if I’m nervous, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else.\n\nThe problem I notice is less like physical restlessness and more… I don’t know, I can be sitting there, but that doesn’t mean I’m fully settled, if that makes sense. But as far as fidgeting all the time or needing to get up and move, I haven’t really paid attention to that as a big issue. It’s not the thing people usually complain about with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. That’s probably one of the biggest things I notice day to day.\n\nLike with the digital work orders, I’ll open one up thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I just need to read the notes and figure out what they’re asking for. And I’ll start reading it, but halfway through, if there’s a noise nearby or someone says something, or even if I just think about the next job I’m supposed to get to, my mind just kind of slips off. Then I’m staring at the screen and I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once, and it’s frustrating because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s like I’m looking at them but they’re not sticking. And then if the phone rings or somebody walks by or there’s some other maintenance issue happening around me, I can lose the thread completely.\n\nIt makes simple things take longer than they should, because I’ll think I understand the work order and then realize I missed a detail in the notes. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted by what’s around me, especially when I’m trying to focus on reading or tracking the steps of a job.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if what I do counts as “trouble organizing” or if it’s just me having a messy way of doing things.\n\nI mean, I can get through my day, and I do have ways I try to keep track of what needs to be done, but it’s not always neat or smooth. Sometimes I feel like I have the tasks in my head, but then when there are several things going on, I’m not always sure what order to do them in or I have to stop and kind of re-figure it out. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt home, too, I wouldn’t say I’m perfectly organized. I’ll start with good intentions, like making a list or putting things in a certain place, and then it doesn’t always stay that way. But I guess I’ve also gotten used to working around it, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal and what’s actually a problem.\n\nSo… maybe? I notice I can feel scattered with tasks, especially if there are a lot of little steps, but I’m not sure I’d know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worrying about something, even when there isn't anything in particular going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about things a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying when nothing is going on.\n\nLike, if there’s an actual issue, then yes, I’ll think about it. But just sitting around feeling worried for no reason… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a constant thing. Sometimes my mind is busy, but busy doesn’t always feel the same as worried, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d need to pay more attention to it. It’s not something I would have come in saying was a big problem, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have feelings of fear or apprehension about what might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often feel fear, exactly. Apprehension… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s in a big, constant way.\n\nLike, I’ll think ahead about what could go wrong with something, but usually there’s some reason for it. If there’s something coming up or something I haven’t figured out yet, then sure, I might feel uneasy. But just generally feeling scared about the future all the time, or like something bad is definitely going to happen… I don’t think that’s really how I experience it.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I don’t always know where the line is between normal thinking ahead and actual worrying. I can get caught up thinking about things, but I wouldn’t have described myself as fearful about the future, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I worry about those things when they’re going well. I mean, I think about work stuff, and money, and people in my life, sure, because those are just things you have to keep in mind. But if nothing is actively wrong, I’m not sure I’m sitting there worrying in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I might still go over things in my head, like whether I paid something or whether I handled something right at work, but I don’t always experience that as worry exactly. It’s more like my mind keeps checking things or trying to keep track. And with relationships, I don’t think I’m constantly afraid something bad is happening if everything seems fine.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe not often? Or at least I haven’t really noticed it as a major thing. I’m having a little trouble separating “normal thinking about everyday responsibilities” from actual worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’m usually worried when things are okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts about potential problems or difficulties in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s specifically potential problems or difficulties, like in a worry sense.\n\nA lot of times it’s more like I’m trying to keep track of what I’m supposed to do, or I’m replaying something because I don’t feel like I have it organized in my head. What was I supposed to do next? Did I miss something?” \n\nI guess that could look like being preoccupied with problems, but emotionally it doesn’t always feel like fear or dread. It’s more just mental noise, if that makes sense. I can get stuck on practical things, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly expecting daily life to go wrong. At least, I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your memory issues or trouble keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. And with relationships, I don’t think I’m constantly afraid something bad is happening if everything seems fine.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe not often? Or at least I haven’t really noticed it as a major thing. I’m having a little trouble separating “normal thinking about everyday responsibilities” from actual worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’m usually worried when things are okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts about potential problems or difficulties in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s specifically potential problems or difficulties, like in a worry sense.\n\nA lot of times it’s more like I’m trying to keep track of what I’m supposed to do, or I’m replaying something because I don’t feel like I have it organized in my head. So if I have a work task or something at home I need to handle, I might keep circling back to it in my mind, but it’s not always, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like, “Did I remember that? What was I supposed to do next? Did I miss something?” \n\nI guess that could look like being preoccupied with problems, but emotionally it doesn’t always feel like fear or dread. It’s more just mental noise, if that makes sense. I can get stuck on practical things, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly expecting daily life to go wrong. At least, I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your memory issues or trouble keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I notice it more at work than at home, but it does affect how people deal with me, if that counts as relationships.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care—I’ll genuinely mean to call someone back or check on something, and then it just slips, or I lose track because three other things came in. Then someone asks me about it later and I’m standing there trying to remember what I even promised.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers and supervisors are frustrated with me, and I can feel people trusting me less. Like they’ll remind me twice, or they’ll ask someone else instead, and that feels awful because I’m not trying to dump things on other people. It’s just piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my chance at moving up. So, yeah, I guess it’s caused problems with people, mostly because they think I’m not following through, when from my side it feels like I’m constantly trying to keep all these pieces from falling through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering things or keeping track of tasks outside of work, like at home or with personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside of work it happens some, but honestly the biggest place it’s showing up—and the reason I’m even sitting here—is work. That’s where it’s become kind of impossible to explain away.\n\nI keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and basic follow-ups I already told people I would handle. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” and in the moment I fully intend to do it. Then something else comes up, or I get pulled in another direction, and it’s just gone until somebody brings it back up. And by then it’s not just a little mistake, it’s another thing on top of all the other things I forgot.\n\nAt home, I do have to keep track of personal stuff too, but I guess I’ve been more focused on the work part because that’s where the consequences are really piling up. It got bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I lose my chance at moving up. So when you ask about remembering things or keeping track of tasks, yes, that’s definitely a problem—I just notice it most because of the scheduled callbacks and work orders and follow-ups where other people are counting on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I worry sometimes like anybody does, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying for no reason exactly.\n\nA lot of what’s in my head feels more like I’m trying to keep things straight, or I’m thinking, “What am I forgetting?” But there’s usually some reason behind it, even if it’s small or messy. Not really, no. I mean… I don’t walk around with this big feeling like something terrible is coming or like the future is doomed. I can get uneasy when I know there are things I’m supposed to handle and I’m not sure I’ve got them all straight, but that feels more tied to whatever is actually in front of me.

That’s probably one of the biggest things I notice day to day.\n\nLike with the digital work orders, I’ll open one up thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I just need to read the notes and figure out what they’re asking for. And I’ll start reading it, but halfway through, if there’s a noise nearby or someone says something, or even if I just think about the next job I’m supposed to get to, my mind just kind of slips off. Then I’m staring at the screen and I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once, and it’s frustrating because it’s not like the words are hard. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted by what’s around me, especially when I’m trying to focus on reading or tracking the steps of a job.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if what I do counts as “trouble organizing” or if it’s just me having a messy way of doing things.\n\nI mean, I can get through my day, and I do have ways I try to keep track of what needs to be done, but it’s not always neat or smooth. Sometimes I feel like I have the tasks in my head, but then when there are several things going on, I’m not always sure what order to do them in or I have to stop and kind of re-figure it out. But if nothing is actively wrong, I’m not sure I’m sitting there worrying in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I might still go over things in my head, like whether I paid something or whether I handled something right at work, but I don’t always experience that as worry exactly. It’s more like my mind keeps checking things or trying to keep track. And with relationships, I don’t think I’m constantly afraid something bad is happening if everything seems fine.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe not often? Or at least I haven’t really noticed it as a major thing. I’m having a little trouble separating “normal thinking about everyday responsibilities” from actual worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’m usually worried when things are okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts about potential problems or difficulties in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s specifically potential problems or difficulties, like in a worry sense.\n\nA lot of times it’s more like I’m trying to keep track of what I’m supposed to do, or I’m replaying something because I don’t feel like I have it organized in my head. So if I have a work task or something at home I need to handle, I might keep circling back to it in my mind, but it’s not always, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like, “Did I remember that? What was I supposed to do next? Did I miss something?” \n\nI guess that could look like being preoccupied with problems, but emotionally it doesn’t always feel like fear or dread. It’s more just mental noise, if that makes sense. I can get stuck on practical things, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly expecting daily life to go wrong. At least, I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your memory issues or trouble keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I notice it more at work than at home, but it does affect how people deal with me, if that counts as relationships.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care—I’ll genuinely mean to call someone back or check on something, and then it just slips, or I lose track because three other things came in. Then someone asks me about it later and I’m standing there trying to remember what I even promised.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers and supervisors are frustrated with me, and I can feel people trusting me less. Like they’ll remind me twice, or they’ll ask someone else instead, and that feels awful because I’m not trying to dump things on other people. It’s just piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my chance at moving up. So, yeah, I guess it’s caused problems with people, mostly because they think I’m not following through, when from my side it feels like I’m constantly trying to keep all these pieces from falling through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering things or keeping track of tasks outside of work, like at home or with personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside of work it happens some, but honestly the biggest place it’s showing up—and the reason I’m even sitting here—is work. That’s where it’s become kind of impossible to explain away.\n\nI keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and basic follow-ups I already told people I would handle. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” and in the moment I fully intend to do it. Then something else comes up, or I get pulled in another direction, and it’s just gone until somebody brings it back up. And by then it’s not just a little mistake, it’s another thing on top of all the other things I forgot.\n\nAt home, I do have to keep track of personal stuff too, but I guess I’ve been more focused on the work part because that’s where the consequences are really piling up. It got bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I lose my chance at moving up. So when you ask about remembering things or keeping track of tasks, yes, that’s definitely a problem—I just notice it most because of the scheduled callbacks and work orders and follow-ups where other people are counting on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I worry sometimes like anybody does, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying for no reason exactly.\n\nA lot of what’s in my head feels more like I’m trying to keep things straight, or I’m thinking, “What am I forgetting?” But there’s usually some reason behind it, even if it’s small or messy. Like I’ll have a sense that something is unfinished, or I’m not sure if I handled something, and then my brain keeps poking at it. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” and in the moment I fully intend to do it. Then something else comes up, or I get pulled in another direction, and it’s just gone until somebody brings it back up. And by then it’s not just a little mistake, it’s another thing on top of all the other things I forgot.\n\nAt home, I do have to keep track of personal stuff too, but I guess I’ve been more focused on the work part because that’s where the consequences are really piling up. It got bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I lose my chance at moving up. So when you ask about remembering things or keeping track of tasks, yes, that’s definitely a problem—I just notice it most because of the scheduled callbacks and work orders and follow-ups where other people are counting on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I worry sometimes like anybody does, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying for no reason exactly.\n\nA lot of what’s in my head feels more like I’m trying to keep things straight, or I’m thinking, “What am I forgetting?” But there’s usually some reason behind it, even if it’s small or messy. Like I’ll have a sense that something is unfinished, or I’m not sure if I handled something, and then my brain keeps poking at it. But I don’t know if that counts as worrying when there’s no obvious reason.\n\nIf you mean, like, just randomly feeling afraid something bad is going to happen when nothing is going on, I haven’t really noticed that as a big thing. It’s more practical stuff, I guess. Or mental clutter. I may be overthinking the question, but I wouldn’t describe myself as constantly worried for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have a general feeling of dread or fear about what's going to happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t walk around with this big feeling like something terrible is coming or like the future is doomed. I can get uneasy when I know there are things I’m supposed to handle and I’m not sure I’ve got them all straight, but that feels more tied to whatever is actually in front of me.\n\nLike if there’s something pending, then yeah, I might have this nagging feeling of, “Okay, what am I missing?” But I wouldn’t call it fear about the future in general. It’s not like I wake up scared about what’s going to happen months from now or anything like that. It’s more that if there’s a situation where I’m expected to keep track of something, I can feel tense because I know I might lose the thread. But general dread… no, I don’t think that’s really how I’d describe it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself worrying about things for weeks or even months at a time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / maintenance_or_repair_worker_general / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not for weeks or months at a time, no. I mean, I can get stuck on something for a while if there’s a real issue going on, but I wouldn’t say I’m carrying the same worry around for months and months with no break.

It’s more like something pops up, I think about it, maybe I circle around it more than I need to for a bit, and then either it gets handled or something else takes over my attention. I’m trying to answer that honestly because I don’t want to just say “no” if I do worry sometimes, but when you say weeks or months, that sounds more constant than what I experience.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of long-running worry. At least not as a main problem.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m here because I keep dropping the ball on things I’m supposed to remember. Like scheduled callbacks, work orders, follow-ups I told people I’d handle—basic stuff that should be routine. And it’s not just one or two things anymore, it’s piled up.\n\nAt work, if somebody asks me to call back or check on something, I really mean to do it, but then it’s like it disappears unless it’s right in front of me. Then later someone says, “Did you ever follow up on that?” and I get that sinking feeling because I know I forgot again. It’s gotten bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I ruin my chance at moving up. I’ve been trying to get ahead, and now I’m worried I’m going to lose that shot because I can’t keep track of what I promised people.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety have you been feeling because of these memory issues?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I probably want to admit. It’s not just, “Oh, I forgot something and I feel bad.” It’s turned into this constant knot in my stomach because now there are actual consequences stacking up.\n\nI’ve got a backlog at work that I keep trying to catch up on, but then more stuff gets added and I miss another callback or forget to close something out, and then tenants complain because they’re waiting on me or they think I ignored them. And honestly, I don’t blame them, but it makes me feel awful because I’m not trying to blow anyone off.\n\nThe worst part is it’s on my record now. There was a safety review tied to something I missed, and that scared me. Like, this isn’t just annoying anymore — it makes me look unreliable. I’ve been trying to move up, trying to show I can handle more responsibility, and now I’m worried I’ve ruined that. I keep thinking, if they’re already watching me for mistakes, why would they ever promote me?\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the anxiety is pretty high. I feel embarrassed, stressed, and kind of scared that all the work I’ve put in is going to get wiped out because I can’t keep track of things the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not new. I mean, the work stuff has made it a lot more obvious and a lot more serious, but if I’m honest, I remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things, but I was always the kid who misplaced papers or didn’t turn something in because I couldn’t find it, or I’d miss one part of the directions even though I thought I was listening. I needed reminders to finish classwork, not because I didn’t care, but because I’d get off track or lose where I was in the assignment.\n\nSo… yeah, this job pressure has made it feel worse, but the remembering and keeping track of things part has been there a long time. I just used to think it was me being careless or scattered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still or is always bouncing around. I might shift in my chair or mess with something in my hands if I’m nervous, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else.\n\nThe problem I notice is less like physical restlessness and more… I don’t know, I can be sitting there, but that doesn’t mean I’m fully settled, if that makes sense. But as far as fidgeting all the time or needing to get up and move, I haven’t really paid attention to that as a big issue. It’s not the thing people usually complain about with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. That’s probably one of the biggest things I notice day to day.\n\nLike with the digital work orders, I’ll open one up thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I just need to read the notes and figure out what they’re asking for. And I’ll start reading it, but halfway through, if there’s a noise nearby or someone says something, or even if I just think about the next job I’m supposed to get to, my mind just kind of slips off. Then I’m staring at the screen and I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once, and it’s frustrating because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s like I’m looking at them but they’re not sticking. And then if the phone rings or somebody walks by or there’s some other maintenance issue happening around me, I can lose the thread completely.\n\nIt makes simple things take longer than they should, because I’ll think I understand the work order and then realize I missed a detail in the notes. So yes, I’d say I’m pretty easily distracted by what’s around me, especially when I’m trying to focus on reading or tracking the steps of a job.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if what I do counts as “trouble organizing” or if it’s just me having a messy way of doing things.\n\nI mean, I can get through my day, and I do have ways I try to keep track of what needs to be done, but it’s not always neat or smooth. Sometimes I feel like I have the tasks in my head, but then when there are several things going on, I’m not always sure what order to do them in or I have to stop and kind of re-figure it out. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt home, too, I wouldn’t say I’m perfectly organized. I’ll start with good intentions, like making a list or putting things in a certain place, and then it doesn’t always stay that way. But I guess I’ve also gotten used to working around it, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal and what’s actually a problem.\n\nSo… maybe? I notice I can feel scattered with tasks, especially if there are a lot of little steps, but I’m not sure I’d know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worrying about something, even when there isn't anything in particular going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about things a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying when nothing is going on.\n\nLike, if there’s an actual issue, then yes, I’ll think about it. But just sitting around feeling worried for no reason… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a constant thing. Sometimes my mind is busy, but busy doesn’t always feel the same as worried, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d need to pay more attention to it. It’s not something I would have come in saying was a big problem, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have feelings of fear or apprehension about what might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often feel fear, exactly. Apprehension… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s in a big, constant way.\n\nLike, I’ll think ahead about what could go wrong with something, but usually there’s some reason for it. If there’s something coming up or something I haven’t figured out yet, then sure, I might feel uneasy. But just generally feeling scared about the future all the time, or like something bad is definitely going to happen… I don’t think that’s really how I experience it.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I don’t always know where the line is between normal thinking ahead and actual worrying. I can get caught up thinking about things, but I wouldn’t have described myself as fearful about the future, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I worry about those things when they’re going well. I mean, I think about work stuff, and money, and people in my life, sure, because those are just things you have to keep in mind. But if nothing is actively wrong, I’m not sure I’m sitting there worrying in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I might still go over things in my head, like whether I paid something or whether I handled something right at work, but I don’t always experience that as worry exactly. It’s more like my mind keeps checking things or trying to keep track. And with relationships, I don’t think I’m constantly afraid something bad is happening if everything seems fine.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe not often? Or at least I haven’t really noticed it as a major thing. I’m having a little trouble separating “normal thinking about everyday responsibilities” from actual worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’m usually worried when things are okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts about potential problems or difficulties in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s specifically potential problems or difficulties, like in a worry sense.\n\nA lot of times it’s more like I’m trying to keep track of what I’m supposed to do, or I’m replaying something because I don’t feel like I have it organized in my head. So if I have a work task or something at home I need to handle, I might keep circling back to it in my mind, but it’s not always, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like, “Did I remember that? What was I supposed to do next? Did I miss something?” \n\nI guess that could look like being preoccupied with problems, but emotionally it doesn’t always feel like fear or dread. It’s more just mental noise, if that makes sense. I can get stuck on practical things, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly expecting daily life to go wrong. At least, I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your memory issues or trouble keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I notice it more at work than at home, but it does affect how people deal with me, if that counts as relationships.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care—I’ll genuinely mean to call someone back or check on something, and then it just slips, or I lose track because three other things came in. Then someone asks me about it later and I’m standing there trying to remember what I even promised.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers and supervisors are frustrated with me, and I can feel people trusting me less. Like they’ll remind me twice, or they’ll ask someone else instead, and that feels awful because I’m not trying to dump things on other people. It’s just piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my chance at moving up. So, yeah, I guess it’s caused problems with people, mostly because they think I’m not following through, when from my side it feels like I’m constantly trying to keep all these pieces from falling through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering things or keeping track of tasks outside of work, like at home or with personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside of work it happens some, but honestly the biggest place it’s showing up—and the reason I’m even sitting here—is work. And by then it’s not just a little mistake, it’s another thing on top of all the other things I forgot.\n\nAt home, I do have to keep track of personal stuff too, but I guess I’ve been more focused on the work part because that’s where the consequences are really piling up. It got bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I lose my chance at moving up. So when you ask about remembering things or keeping track of tasks, yes, that’s definitely a problem—I just notice it most because of the scheduled callbacks and work orders and follow-ups where other people are counting on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. But just generally feeling scared about the future all the time, or like something bad is definitely going to happen… I don’t think that’s really how I experience it.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I don’t always know where the line is between normal thinking ahead and actual worrying. I can get caught up thinking about things, but I wouldn’t have described myself as fearful about the future, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I worry about those things when they’re going well. I mean, I think about work stuff, and money, and people in my life, sure, because those are just things you have to keep in mind. But if nothing is actively wrong, I’m not sure I’m sitting there worrying in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I might still go over things in my head, like whether I paid something or whether I handled something right at work, but I don’t always experience that as worry exactly. It’s more like my mind keeps checking things or trying to keep track. And with relationships, I don’t think I’m constantly afraid something bad is happening if everything seems fine.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe not often? Or at least I haven’t really noticed it as a major thing. I’m having a little trouble separating “normal thinking about everyday responsibilities” from actual worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’m usually worried when things are okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts about potential problems or difficulties in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s specifically potential problems or difficulties, like in a worry sense.\n\nA lot of times it’s more like I’m trying to keep track of what I’m supposed to do, or I’m replaying something because I don’t feel like I have it organized in my head. So if I have a work task or something at home I need to handle, I might keep circling back to it in my mind, but it’s not always, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like, “Did I remember that? What was I supposed to do next? Did I miss something?” \n\nI guess that could look like being preoccupied with problems, but emotionally it doesn’t always feel like fear or dread. It’s more just mental noise, if that makes sense. I can get stuck on practical things, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly expecting daily life to go wrong. At least, I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your memory issues or trouble keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I notice it more at work than at home, but it does affect how people deal with me, if that counts as relationships.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care—I’ll genuinely mean to call someone back or check on something, and then it just slips, or I lose track because three other things came in. Then someone asks me about it later and I’m standing there trying to remember what I even promised.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers and supervisors are frustrated with me, and I can feel people trusting me less. Like they’ll remind me twice, or they’ll ask someone else instead, and that feels awful because I’m not trying to dump things on other people. It’s just piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my chance at moving up. So, yeah, I guess it’s caused problems with people, mostly because they think I’m not following through, when from my side it feels like I’m constantly trying to keep all these pieces from falling through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering things or keeping track of tasks outside of work, like at home or with personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside of work it happens some, but honestly the biggest place it’s showing up—and the reason I’m even sitting here—is work. That’s where it’s become kind of impossible to explain away.\n\nI keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and basic follow-ups I already told people I would handle. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” and in the moment I fully intend to do it. Then something else comes up, or I get pulled in another direction, and it’s just gone until somebody brings it back up. And by then it’s not just a little mistake, it’s another thing on top of all the other things I forgot.\n\nAt home, I do have to keep track of personal stuff too, but I guess I’ve been more focused on the work part because that’s where the consequences are really piling up. It got bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I lose my chance at moving up. I’d say it’s more the focusing and keeping track part, honestly. I mean, I can feel overwhelmed after it starts piling up, but that’s not what seems to start it.

What brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and those little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And they’re not always big complicated things. Sometimes it’s just, “Call this person back,” or “Check on that work order,” and I’ll mean to do it, I’ll even be sure I’m going to remember, and then it just drops out. Then later something reminds me and I get that awful feeling like, “Oh no, I never did that.”

And now it’s piled up enough that work basically sent me here before I mess up my chance at moving up. So I do get stressed about it, but it feels like the stress comes after I realize I missed something again. It’s not like I’m so anxious that I can’t remember. It’s more like I don’t hold onto the task unless it’s right in front of me, and then by the time I remember, it’s already a problem.

I mean, the work stuff has made it a lot more obvious and a lot more serious, but if I’m honest, I remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things, but I was always the kid who misplaced papers or didn’t turn something in because I couldn’t find it, or I’d miss one part of the directions even though I thought I was listening. I needed reminders to finish classwork, not because I didn’t care, but because I’d get off track or lose where I was in the assignment.\n\nSo… yeah, this job pressure has made it feel worse, but the remembering and keeping track of things part has been there a long time. I just used to think it was me being careless or scattered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. That’s probably one of the biggest things I notice day to day.\n\nLike with the digital work orders, I’ll open one up thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I just need to read the notes and figure out what they’re asking for. And I’ll start reading it, but halfway through, if there’s a noise nearby or someone says something, or even if I just think about the next job I’m supposed to get to, my mind just kind of slips off. Then I’m staring at the screen and I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once, and it’s frustrating because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s like I’m looking at them but they’re not sticking. Sometimes I feel like I have the tasks in my head, but then when there are several things going on, I’m not always sure what order to do them in or I have to stop and kind of re-figure it out. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt home, too, I wouldn’t say I’m perfectly organized. I’ll start with good intentions, like making a list or putting things in a certain place, and then it doesn’t always stay that way. But I guess I’ve also gotten used to working around it, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal and what’s actually a problem.\n\nSo… maybe? I mean, I do think about things a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying when nothing is going on.\n\nLike, if there’s an actual issue, then yes, I’ll think about it. But just sitting around feeling worried for no reason… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a constant thing. Sometimes my mind is busy, but busy doesn’t always feel the same as worried, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d need to pay more attention to it. It’s not something I would have come in saying was a big problem, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have feelings of fear or apprehension about what might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often feel fear, exactly. Apprehension… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s in a big, constant way.\n\nLike, I’ll think ahead about what could go wrong with something, but usually there’s some reason for it. If there’s something coming up or something I haven’t figured out yet, then sure, I might feel uneasy. But just generally feeling scared about the future all the time, or like something bad is definitely going to happen… I don’t think that’s really how I experience it.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because I don’t always know where the line is between normal thinking ahead and actual worrying. I can get caught up thinking about things, but I wouldn’t have described myself as fearful about the future, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I worry about those things when they’re going well. I mean, I think about work stuff, and money, and people in my life, sure, because those are just things you have to keep in mind. But if nothing is actively wrong, I’m not sure I’m sitting there worrying in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I might still go over things in my head, like whether I paid something or whether I handled something right at work, but I don’t always experience that as worry exactly. It’s more like my mind keeps checking things or trying to keep track. And with relationships, I don’t think I’m constantly afraid something bad is happening if everything seems fine.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe not often? Or at least I haven’t really noticed it as a major thing. I’m having a little trouble separating “normal thinking about everyday responsibilities” from actual worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’m usually worried when things are okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently preoccupied with thoughts about potential problems or difficulties in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do get preoccupied with things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s specifically potential problems or difficulties, like in a worry sense.\n\nA lot of times it’s more like I’m trying to keep track of what I’m supposed to do, or I’m replaying something because I don’t feel like I have it organized in my head. So if I have a work task or something at home I need to handle, I might keep circling back to it in my mind, but it’s not always, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like, “Did I remember that? What was I supposed to do next? Did I miss something?” \n\nI guess that could look like being preoccupied with problems, but emotionally it doesn’t always feel like fear or dread. It’s more just mental noise, if that makes sense. I can get stuck on practical things, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly expecting daily life to go wrong. At least, I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your memory issues or trouble keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I notice it more at work than at home, but it does affect how people deal with me, if that counts as relationships.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care—I’ll genuinely mean to call someone back or check on something, and then it just slips, or I lose track because three other things came in. Then someone asks me about it later and I’m standing there trying to remember what I even promised.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers and supervisors are frustrated with me, and I can feel people trusting me less. Like they’ll remind me twice, or they’ll ask someone else instead, and that feels awful because I’m not trying to dump things on other people. It’s just piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my chance at moving up. So, yeah, I guess it’s caused problems with people, mostly because they think I’m not following through, when from my side it feels like I’m constantly trying to keep all these pieces from falling through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering things or keeping track of tasks outside of work, like at home or with personal activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside of work it happens some, but honestly the biggest place it’s showing up—and the reason I’m even sitting here—is work. That’s where it’s become kind of impossible to explain away.\n\nI keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and basic follow-ups I already told people I would handle. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” and in the moment I fully intend to do it. Then something else comes up, or I get pulled in another direction, and it’s just gone until somebody brings it back up. And by then it’s not just a little mistake, it’s another thing on top of all the other things I forgot.\n\nAt home, I do have to keep track of personal stuff too, but I guess I’ve been more focused on the work part because that’s where the consequences are really piling up. It got bad enough that work sent me here, basically, before I lose my chance at moving up. So when you ask about remembering things or keeping track of tasks, yes, that’s definitely a problem—I just notice it most because of the scheduled callbacks and work orders and follow-ups where other people are counting on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I worry sometimes like anybody does, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying for no reason exactly.\n\nA lot of what’s in my head feels more like I’m trying to keep things straight, or I’m thinking, “What am I forgetting?” But there’s usually some reason behind it, even if it’s small or messy. Like I’ll have a sense that something is unfinished, or I’m not sure if I handled something, and then my brain keeps poking at it. But I don’t know if that counts as worrying when there’s no obvious reason.\n\nIf you mean, like, just randomly feeling afraid something bad is going to happen when nothing is going on, I haven’t really noticed that as a big thing. It’s more practical stuff, I guess. Or mental clutter. I may be overthinking the question, but I wouldn’t describe myself as constantly worried for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have a general feeling of dread or fear about what's going to happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t walk around with this big feeling like something terrible is coming or like the future is doomed. I can get uneasy when I know there are things I’m supposed to handle and I’m not sure I’ve got them all straight, but that feels more tied to whatever is actually in front of me.\n\nLike if there’s something pending, then yeah, I might have this nagging feeling of, “Okay, what am I missing?” But I wouldn’t call it fear about the future in general. It’s not like I wake up scared about what’s going to happen months from now or anything like that. It’s more that if there’s a situation where I’m expected to keep track of something, I can feel tense because I know I might lose the thread. But general dread… no, I don’t think that’s really how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for weeks or even months at a time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for weeks or months at a time, no. I mean, I can get stuck on something for a while if there’s a real issue going on, but I wouldn’t say I’m carrying the same worry around for months and months with no break.\n\nIt’s more like something pops up, I think about it, maybe I circle around it more than I need to for a bit, and then either it gets handled or something else takes over my attention. I’m trying to answer that honestly because I don’t want to just say “no” if I do worry sometimes, but when you say weeks or months, that sounds more constant than what I experience.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of long-running worry. At least not as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with remembering tasks and appointments is more related to feeling overwhelmed or anxious, or to having trouble focusing and paying attention?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the focusing and keeping track part, honestly. I mean, I can feel overwhelmed after it starts piling up, but that’s not what seems to start it.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and those little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And they’re not always big complicated things. Sometimes it’s just, “Call this person back,” or “Check on that work order,” and I’ll mean to do it, I’ll even be sure I’m going to remember, and then it just drops out. Then later something reminds me and I get that awful feeling like, “Oh no, I never did that.”\n\nAnd now it’s piled up enough that work basically sent me here before I mess up my chance at moving up. So I do get stressed about it, but it feels like the stress comes after I realize I missed something again. It’s not like I’m so anxious that I can’t remember. It’s more like I don’t hold onto the task unless it’s right in front of me, and then by the time I remember, it’s already a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the paying attention and keeping track part, not anxiety first. I mean, I do get anxious after the fact, because by then I’m realizing, “Oh, I forgot that callback,” or “I never followed up on that work order,” and then it’s already become a bigger deal than it needed to be.

What brought me in is really that pattern. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, even basic follow-ups that I promised people I would do. They’re not always complicated things, either, which makes it more frustrating. It’ll be something simple that I genuinely intend to handle, and then it’s like it just slips off the list in my head unless something puts it right in front of me again.

And now it’s piled up so much at work that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up. So yes, I’m anxious about forgetting now, because there are consequences, but I don’t think the anxiety is what causes it. I think I’m missing the details and losing track, and then the anxiety comes afterward when I see what I’ve missed.

Sometimes it’s just, “Call this person back,” or “Check on that work order,” and I’ll mean to do it, I’ll even be sure I’m going to remember, and then it just drops out. Then later something reminds me and I get that awful feeling like, “Oh no, I never did that.”\n\nAnd now it’s piled up enough that work basically sent me here before I mess up my chance at moving up. So I do get stressed about it, but it feels like the stress comes after I realize I missed something again. It’s not like I’m so anxious that I can’t remember. It’s more like I don’t hold onto the task unless it’s right in front of me, and then by the time I remember, it’s already a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness and difficulty keeping track of tasks is more because you have trouble paying attention to details or because you get anxious about forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention and keeping track part, not anxiety first. I mean, I do get anxious after the fact, because by then I’m realizing, “Oh, I forgot that callback,” or “I never followed up on that work order,” and then it’s already become a bigger deal than it needed to be.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that pattern. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, even basic follow-ups that I promised people I would do. They’re not always complicated things, either, which makes it more frustrating. It’ll be something simple that I genuinely intend to handle, and then it’s like it just slips off the list in my head unless something puts it right in front of me again.\n\nAnd now it’s piled up so much at work that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up. So yes, I’m anxious about forgetting now, because there are consequences, but I don’t think the anxiety is what causes it. I think I’m missing the details and losing track, and then the anxiety comes afterward when I see what I’ve missed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m trying to answer that honestly because I don’t want to just say “no” if I do worry sometimes, but when you say weeks or months, that sounds more constant than what I experience.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of long-running worry. At least not as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with remembering tasks and appointments is more related to feeling overwhelmed or anxious, or to having trouble focusing and paying attention?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the focusing and keeping track part, honestly. I mean, I can feel overwhelmed after it starts piling up, but that’s not what seems to start it.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and those little follow-ups I told people I’d do. And they’re not always big complicated things. Sometimes it’s just, “Call this person back,” or “Check on that work order,” and I’ll mean to do it, I’ll even be sure I’m going to remember, and then it just drops out. Then later something reminds me and I get that awful feeling like, “Oh no, I never did that.”\n\nAnd now it’s piled up enough that work basically sent me here before I mess up my chance at moving up. So I do get stressed about it, but it feels like the stress comes after I realize I missed something again. It’s not like I’m so anxious that I can’t remember. It’s more like I don’t hold onto the task unless it’s right in front of me, and then by the time I remember, it’s already a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness and difficulty keeping track of tasks is more because you have trouble paying attention to details or because you get anxious about forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention and keeping track part, not anxiety first. I mean, I do get anxious after the fact, because by then I’m realizing, “Oh, I forgot that callback,” or “I never followed up on that work order,” and then it’s already become a bigger deal than it needed to be.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that pattern. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, even basic follow-ups that I promised people I would do. They’re not always complicated things, either, which makes it more frustrating. It’ll be something simple that I genuinely intend to handle, and then it’s like it just slips off the list in my head unless something puts it right in front of me again.\n\nAnd now it’s piled up so much at work that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up. So yes, I’m anxious about forgetting now, because there are consequences, but I don’t think the anxiety is what causes it. I think I’m missing the details and losing track, and then the anxiety comes afterward when I see what I’ve missed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your main problem is that you have trouble paying attention to things or that you worry about forgetting them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / maintenance_or_repair_worker_general / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I can start a digital work order thinking I’ve got it, but halfway through reading the notes my mind slips off to the next noise or job and I realize I don’t know what I just looked at.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more that I have trouble paying attention to it in the first place, or staying with it long enough. The worrying comes later, when I realize I missed something again.

Like, I can open up a digital work order and honestly think, “Okay, I’ve got this.” I’ll start reading the notes, and halfway through, my attention just kind of slips off. It might be a noise nearby, or another job popping into my head, or someone moving around, and then I look back at the screen and realize I don’t actually know what I just read. I was looking at it, but I wasn’t really taking it in.

And then I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once, which is embarrassing because it’s not like the notes are always that complicated. It’s just that I don’t stay locked onto them. So I wouldn’t say the main issue is that I’m sitting there worrying, “What if I forget?” It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto the thing unless I keep forcing it back, and then afterward I worry because I know I may have missed something important.

Like I’ll have a sense that something is unfinished, or I’m not sure if I handled something, and then my brain keeps poking at it.
